What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess work got scared. I’m a waitress, and during service I’ve been sort of freezing, like just stopping in the middle of everything. One time I was by the soda station holding the cash drawer key, and I was just saying table numbers over and over instead of closing out the checks.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because restaurants are already chaos, you know, like everybody yelling for ranch and refills, and the printer makes that noise—anyway, people noticed I wasn’t doing things in the right order. That’s why I’m here, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do these freezing episodes happen only at work or have you experienced them in other situations as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly at work, I think. That’s where people really saw it, because service is so fast and everything has to happen in the right order. I’d just… stop. Like I said, by the soda station with the cash drawer key in my hand, saying table numbers over and over instead of closing checks.\n\nAnd then someone’s asking for lemons, and the printer’s spitting tickets, and I’m thinking about whether table 14 was actually 41—sorry, that’s not what you asked. But yeah, the scary ones people noticed were at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed right before these freezing episodes happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly. Maybe overwhelmed, but work is always overwhelming, so it’s hard to tell what’s normal and what’s the… freezing thing.\n\nLike, right before it happens, there’s usually a lot going on—plates in the window, someone wants separate checks, and I’m trying to remember who had iced tea versus Diet Coke. And then I start thinking about the order of things, like if I close the check first or get the sauce first, and then I’m just stuck. But I don’t really notice a feeling right before, not like, “oh, I’m panicking now.” It’s more like everything gets too tangled.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding going to work because of these freezing episodes?\n\nPatient: I mean… I haven’t exactly been calling out sick just because of it, but I do dread going in now. Especially because they were talking about making me shift lead, and now I can tell that’s slipping away because I keep messing up the order of things and confusing people during service and closing.\n\nIt’s not even just work, though—sorry, I know you asked about work. At home my husband says I’m barely there with him anymore, like I’m sitting right there but not really present. So… yeah, I guess I’m avoiding it in my head, even if I still show up sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're outside of your body or watching yourself when these freezing episodes happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think of it like I’m floating above myself or anything. It’s more like… I get stuck in the middle of doing something and then I’m trying to catch up to what everyone is saying.\n\nLike when the trays are lined up and someone calls my name, I can hear it, but then I’m thinking, “Was I going to table 12 or did table 12 already pay?” And then I start remembering this one night where the lemon wedges were in the wrong bin—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed watching myself from outside. It’s more just… blank or tangled, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… sad, I guess? Not like crying all day or anything. It’s more like I get worn out, and then I can’t tell if that’s sad or just being tired from everything piling up.\n\nLike yesterday I was trying to fold laundry and I kept matching the wrong socks, and then I remembered this old apartment we had where the dryer used to leave everything damp—sorry, that’s not really the question. I guess I’ve felt kind of low, but I don’t know if I’d say most of the time. It comes and goes, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I get uncomfortable, sure, but “intense fear” sounds like… like something really dramatic, like in a movie where someone can’t breathe in an elevator. I don’t think it’s like that.\n\nSometimes things do kind of rush up on me, but then I start thinking about whether I missed something at work, or if I left the stove knob turned the right way—well, not the stove, we have that weird burner that clicks if it’s not lined up right. Sorry. I guess I’m not sure if it peaks in minutes. I haven’t really timed it or thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that seemed to come out of nowhere?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. “Out of nowhere” is hard, because usually I can find some reason if I start picking at it, like maybe the restaurant was too loud or someone was waiting too long, or I forgot where I put my order pad.\n\nBut then sometimes I’ll feel weird all at once, like everything gets too much, and I think, wait, did something happen? And then I’m thinking about the ice machine at work because it makes this clunking sound right before it jams—sorry, that’s not really it.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call it sudden intense fear. Discomfort, maybe, but I can’t really say it comes from nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're disconnected from yourself or your surroundings when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Disconnected… I’m not sure. Like, do you mean like watching myself from outside? I don’t think I’ve noticed that exactly.\n\nSometimes places feel kind of too bright or too loud, like at work when the plates are clattering and someone’s asking for extra ranch and I’m trying to remember table numbers—then I sort of lose track of what I’m doing. But I don’t know if that’s being disconnected or just getting overwhelmed.\n\nSo… maybe not? I’m not really sure what counts.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding specific tasks or situations at work because of these freezing episodes?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, sort of. I don’t really avoid the whole job, but I try not to be the one closing checks if it’s really busy, because that’s when it happened and everyone got scared.\n\nI’ll just freeze in the middle of service, like I’m standing by the soda station with the cash drawer key in my hand, and instead of doing the checks I’m saying the table numbers over and over. Or I do things out of order, like I’ll go to grab ranch before I even put the order in, and then I’m just stuck there thinking about table 12, table 4, table 12… sorry.\n\nSo I guess I do avoid some parts, because I don’t want people staring at me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like things around you aren't real when you're in the middle of one of these freezing episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “not real” sounds like a movie thing to me, like when the background goes blurry or something. I don’t think I’ve had that exactly.\n\nWhen I freeze, it’s more like everything is still there, but I can’t make myself move right, or I get stuck on one thing. Like the register screen, the little buttons, the receipt paper curling up—actually the receipt paper always does that if the roll is in wrong, and then people yank it and it jams.\n\nSorry. Um… I haven’t really noticed things seeming unreal. Just overwhelming, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel nervous or anxious when you're around your coworkers or other people at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get nervous around them. Not everybody, but a couple of the servers, and there’s this one regular customer who comes in and sits where he can see the register, and I feel like they’re watching how I ring things in.\n\nLike if a ticket has something crossed out or there’s a weird note on it, I’ll think, okay, that’s not an accident, that’s a sign they’re leaving to show I messed up so they can get me fired. My husband says restaurants are just messy and tickets get messed up all the time, and maybe he’s right, but when I’m there it feels… it feels like they’re trying to prove something about me.\n\nAnd then I’m anxious, yeah, because I can’t just act normal when I feel like they’re checking every button I press.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a dream or detached from the world around you when these freezing episodes happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really think of it like a dream, exactly. I mean, I know I’m at work, I know the floor is sticky by the drink station and the trays are stacked wrong half the time—sorry, that’s not what you asked.\n\nIt’s more like I get stuck, and everything gets kind of too much, but I don’t know if I’d call it detached from the world. I haven’t really noticed that feeling, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your freezing episodes are triggered by a specific object, situation, or activity at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s one specific object, like not just the register or the soda machine. It’s more during service, when there’s a lot going on and checks need closing and people are waiting.\n\nLike, what scared everyone was I’d just… freeze by the soda station holding the cash drawer key, and instead of closing checks I’d be saying table numbers over and over. Or I’d do things out of order, like go to grab receipts before I even rang the order in. The soda station is always a mess too, the syrup boxes leak sometimes and then your shoes stick—sorry.\n\nSo, maybe it’s the middle of service, that whole situation, more than one thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your freezing episodes would happen in other busy or chaotic situations outside of work?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I don’t know. Busy places do make me feel scrambled, but the real freezing that scared everybody was at work, in the middle of service.\n\nLike I’d be standing by the soda station holding the cash drawer key, and I was supposed to be closing checks, but instead I’d just keep saying table numbers over and over. Or I’d do things backward, like reaching for receipts before I even rang in the order. And then someone would be asking for iced tea refills and the printer would be spitting out tickets—sorry, that’s not really outside work.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if it would happen somewhere else the same way. I mostly know it happens when service gets chaotic.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a physical sensation, like a racing heart or sweating, right before one of these freezing episodes happened?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed my heart or sweating right before. Maybe I’m not paying attention to that part because everything is already moving too fast, like people talking over each other and the tickets coming out and someone asking where the extra napkins went.\n\nSometimes I’m just suddenly stuck, like I missed the step where I was supposed to realize it was coming. I don’t think I can say there’s a body feeling first. I’d have to watch for it, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense fear or discomfort that seemed to come out of nowhere and reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think of it like fear coming out of nowhere exactly… it’s more like everything gets too much, and then I’m not tracking what I’m doing.\n\nLike, people say “out of nowhere,” but at the restaurant there’s always something before it—the printer, people waiting, someone asking me where the lemons are, and then I’m thinking about how the lemon bin never gets filled unless I do it, which is not really what you asked.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’ve had that sudden fear that peaks in minutes. I haven’t really noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack, which is a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a panic attack. I’ve heard people say that, like it’s this big rush of fear, but I don’t really track it that way.\n\nWhen things get loud or too much, I just sort of… lose the thread. And then I’m thinking about the next thing I was supposed to do, or what someone asked me, and it’s like the question slips away. So I’m not sure if there’s a peak in minutes or if it’s just me getting overwhelmed. I haven’t noticed it as “a panic attack,” I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a feeling of intense fear or discomfort that seemed to come out of nowhere and was accompanied by physical symptoms like a racing heart or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I’ve been uncomfortable, yeah, but I don’t remember it being like suddenly my heart is racing or I can’t breathe or something. Maybe I just don’t notice that part.\n\nSometimes when I’m at work and everybody needs something at once, it feels like the whole room is leaning in on me, but then I start thinking about the tables and whether I forgot someone’s coffee, and there was this one regular who always asks for extra creamers even though she doesn’t use them—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… no, I can’t say I’ve noticed intense fear out of nowhere with those physical things. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your freezing episodes are more related to feeling overwhelmed and disconnected from what's happening around you, or to a specific fear of something bad happening?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the overwhelmed and disconnected thing, not like I’m afraid a bomb is going to go off or something. Maybe I just don’t notice that part.\n\nSometimes when I’m at work and everybody needs something at once, it feels like the whole room is leaning in on me, but then I start thinking about the tables and whether I forgot someone’s coffee, and there was this one regular who always asks for extra creamers even though she doesn’t use them—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… no, I can’t say I’ve noticed intense fear out of nowhere with those physical things. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your freezing episodes are more related to feeling overwhelmed and disconnected from what's happening around you, or to a specific fear of something bad happening?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the overwhelmed and disconnected thing, not like I’m afraid a bomb is going to go off or something. It’s just… everything stops lining up.\n\nWhat scared everyone at work was I’d freeze right in the middle of service. Or I’d do things in the wrong order, like I was standing by the soda station holding the cash drawer key, and I kept repeating table numbers instead of closing the checks. And people were waiting, and someone needed ranch, and then I’m saying “twelve, four, twelve…” like that helps anything.\n\nSo I don’t know if it’s fear exactly. It feels more like I’m not in the same place as what’s happening around me."
